---
title: "Fast sampling: DDIM, ODE solvers and distillation"
description: "Why DDPM sampling needs T sequential network evaluations and three ways around it: DDIM's deterministic sampler on a subsequence of steps (same trained model), higher-order solvers for the probability-flow ODE (DPM-Solver, EDM's Heun sampler) counted in network evaluations (NFE), and training a few-step model (progressive distillation, consistency models, latent consistency models) — with the published numbers and a numpy comparison on the toy DDPM."
tags: [topic, diffusion]
aliases: ["DDIM", "denoising diffusion implicit model", "NFE", "number of function evaluations", "DPM-Solver", "EDM sampler", "Heun sampler", "consistency model", "progressive distillation", "eta"]
---

*Beyond the lectures · Song, Meng & Ermon (2020) and follow-ups · [[8-diffusion-models/index|Diffusion models]] · builds on [[8-diffusion-models/ddpm|DDPM]] and [[8-diffusion-models/reverse-time-sde-and-score-matching|the reverse-time SDE and probability-flow ODE]]*

> [!abstract] In one breath
> A trained DDPM needs one network evaluation per step and $T = 1000$ steps, in sequence. **DDIM** observes that the DDPM loss only constrains the marginals $q(x_t\mid x_0)$, so the same network can drive a different, *deterministic* reverse process that skips most steps: 10–100 steps instead of 1000, no retraining. That deterministic process is Euler's method on an ODE (the **probability-flow ODE**), so better ODE solvers — **DPM-Solver**, **EDM**'s Heun sampler — reach the same quality in about 10–35 network evaluations. Going further requires training: **progressive distillation** halves the step count repeatedly (down to 4), and **consistency models** learn to jump from noise to data in one step.

## What it is

The cost of sampling a diffusion model is measured in **NFE** — the number of function (network) evaluations per sample — because one forward pass of a large U-Net or transformer dwarfs everything else in a step. Ancestral DDPM sampling ([[8-diffusion-models/ddpm#algorithms-1-and-2|Algorithm 2]]) costs $\text{NFE} = T = 1000$, and the evaluations cannot run in parallel. The DDIM paper puts numbers on it: about 20 hours to sample 50k $32\times32$ images on one RTX 2080 Ti, and nearly 1000 hours for 50k $256\times256$ images, against under a minute for a GAN.

There are three families of fixes:

1. **A better sampler for the same model** — DDIM, and higher-order ODE solvers. No retraining.
2. **A better-trained model that tolerates fewer steps** — e.g. learned variances (Nichol & Dhariwal), EDM's training changes.
3. **Distill the sampler into a new network** — progressive distillation, consistency models, latent consistency models. Retraining (from the teacher), but 1–8 steps.

## What you should know

- Why DDPM sampling is slow: $T$ sequential NFE, and that NFE (not steps) is the fair cost measure.
- The DDIM update: predict $\hat x_0$, then move to $t-1$ along the predicted noise direction, with optional fresh noise of size $\sigma_t(\eta)$; $\eta = 1$ recovers DDPM-like sampling, $\eta = 0$ is deterministic.
- DDIM needs **no retraining**: the training objective only depends on the marginals $q(x_t\mid x_0)$, which all these processes share. It can run on any subsequence $\tau$ of the training steps.
- Deterministic sampling makes $x_T$ a latent code: same $x_T$, similar image at any step count; interpolation and encoding become possible.
- DDIM is the Euler method (and exactly DPM-Solver-1) on the probability-flow ODE; higher-order solvers cut the error per step, at more NFE per step.
- Representative numbers: DDIM CIFAR-10 FID 4.67 at 50 steps vs 4.04 at 1000; DPM-Solver 4.70 at 10 NFE and 2.87 at 20; EDM 1.97 (unconditional) at 35 NFE; progressive distillation 3.0 at 4 steps; consistency models 3.55 at 1 step.
- Distillation trades training compute for sampling speed; the student is a new network.

## DDIM: same model, different sampler

**The key observation.** The DDPM objective $L_\gamma = \sum_t \gamma_t\,\mathbb E\lVert\epsilon_\theta(\sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}x_0 + \sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}\epsilon, t) - \epsilon\rVert^2$ only involves the marginals $q(x_t\mid x_0)$, not how the steps are chained together. Song, Meng & Ermon construct a family of **non-Markovian** forward processes $q_\sigma$, indexed by a vector $\sigma$, that all have the same marginals and lead to the same training objective (their Theorem 1). A network trained as a DDPM is therefore also a trained model for every member of the family — we may pick whichever reverse process samples fastest.

**The update.** Written in DDPM's notation ($\bar\alpha_t$; see the notation trap below), one DDIM step from $x_t$ to $x_{t-1}$ is (DDIM eq. 12):

$$
x_{t-1} = \sqrt{\bar\alpha_{t-1}}\,\underbrace{\frac{x_t - \sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}\,\epsilon_\theta(x_t, t)}{\sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}}}_{\text{predicted } x_0}\;+\;\underbrace{\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_{t-1}-\sigma_t^2}\;\epsilon_\theta(x_t, t)}_{\text{direction pointing to } x_t}\;+\;\underbrace{\sigma_t\,\epsilon_t}_{\text{random noise}}
$$

with $\epsilon_t\sim\mathcal N(0, I)$ and $\bar\alpha_0 := 1$. Read it as: guess the clean image, then re-noise it to level $t-1$, reusing the network's own noise estimate for most of the noise. The amount of fresh noise is set by one knob $\eta$ (DDIM eq. 16):

$$
\sigma_t(\eta) = \eta\,\sqrt{\frac{1-\bar\alpha_{t-1}}{1-\bar\alpha_t}}\,\sqrt{1-\frac{\bar\alpha_t}{\bar\alpha_{t-1}}}.
$$

- $\eta = 1$: for consecutive steps $\sigma_t^2 = \tilde\beta_t$, the DDPM posterior variance, and the update is a DDPM step.
- $\eta = 0$: no noise at all. Given $x_T$, the whole chain is deterministic — the **denoising diffusion implicit model**.

**Skipping steps.** Because only the marginals matter, the same update works between any two training steps: choose an increasing subsequence $\tau = (\tau_1, \dots, \tau_S)$ of $1..T$ and replace $t \to \tau_i$, $t-1 \to \tau_{i-1}$. With $S = 50$ the sampler makes 50 network evaluations instead of 1000.

The toy implementation (tools/diffusion_toy.py, trimmed), used for the figures below:

```python
def sample_ddim(params, n, rng, S, eta=0.0, x_T=None):
    """DDIM eq. 12; eta=1 is DDPM-like."""
    tau = timesteps(S)                                       # e.g. 1, 12, ..., 100
    x = rng.standard_normal((n, 2)) if x_T is None else x_T.copy()
    for i in range(len(tau) - 1, -1, -1):
        t, t_prev = tau[i], (tau[i - 1] if i > 0 else 0)
        ab, ab_prev = _ab(t), _ab(t_prev)                    # alpha_bar, with alpha_bar_0 = 1
        eps = forward(params, x, np.full(n, t))
        x0_hat = (x - np.sqrt(1 - ab) * eps) / np.sqrt(ab)    # predicted x0
        sigma = eta * np.sqrt((1 - ab_prev) / (1 - ab) * (1 - ab / ab_prev))
        dir_xt = np.sqrt(np.maximum(1 - ab_prev - sigma ** 2, 0)) * eps
        noise = sigma * rng.standard_normal(x.shape) if t_prev > 0 else 0.0
        x = np.sqrt(ab_prev) * x0_hat + dir_xt + noise
    return x
```

**Published results** (DDIM Table 1, CIFAR-10 $32\times32$, FID, lower is better; the same model trained with $T = 1000$, sampled with $S$ steps):

| $S$ (steps = NFE) | 10 | 20 | 50 | 100 | 1000 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| $\eta = 0$ (DDIM) | **13.36** | **6.84** | **4.67** | **4.16** | 4.04 |
| $\eta = 0.5$ | 16.66 | 8.35 | 5.25 | 4.46 | 4.29 |
| $\eta = 1$ (DDPM-like) | 41.07 | 18.36 | 8.01 | 5.78 | 4.73 |
| $\hat\sigma$ (Ho et al.'s CIFAR-10 sampler) | 367.43 | 133.37 | 32.72 | 9.99 | **3.17** |

Less noise is better at every step count, with one exception: at the full 1000 steps Ho et al.'s $\hat\sigma$ sampler (3.17) beats DDIM (4.04). With few steps DDIM wins by a wide margin. The paper summarizes it as samples comparable to 1000-step quality within 20–100 steps, a **10× to 50× speed-up**. Two further properties follow from determinism: images generated from the same $x_T$ with 20 or 1000 steps share their high-level features (so $x_T$ acts as a latent code that can be interpolated), and running the ODE backwards *encodes* an image into $x_T$ — the reconstruction error on CIFAR-10 falls from 0.014 at $S = 10$ to 0.0001 at $S = 1000$ (DDIM Table 2, per-dimension MSE on $[0, 1]$).

## DDIM is an ODE solver

Divide the $\eta = 0$ update by $\sqrt{\bar\alpha_{t-1}}$ and write $\bar x := x/\sqrt{\bar\alpha}$ and $\sigma := \sqrt{(1-\bar\alpha)/\bar\alpha}$ (DDIM eq. 13):

$$
\bar x_{t-1} = \bar x_t + (\sigma_{t-1} - \sigma_t)\,\epsilon_\theta(x_t, t),
$$

which is one **Euler step** of the ODE $\dfrac{d\bar x}{d\sigma} = \epsilon_\theta\Bigl(\dfrac{\bar x}{\sqrt{\sigma^2+1}},\ \sigma\Bigr)$ (DDIM eq. 14). Song et al. (2021) derived the same object from the SDE side: for a forward SDE $dx = f(x,t)\,dt + g(t)\,dw$, the **probability-flow ODE**

$$
\frac{dx}{dt} = f(x, t) - \tfrac12\,g(t)^2\,\nabla_x\log p_t(x)
$$

has the same marginal densities $p_t$ as the SDE but no noise ([[8-diffusion-models/reverse-time-sde-and-score-matching|derivation and context]]), and the DDIM paper shows (Proposition 1) that its ODE is the probability-flow ODE of the variance-exploding SDE. Since $\nabla_x\log p_t \approx -\epsilon_\theta/\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}$, a trained $\epsilon_\theta$ is all the ODE needs.

Once sampling is "solve an ODE from $\sigma_{\max}$ to 0", the question becomes numerical analysis: Euler's method has global error $O(h)$ in the step size $h$, so halving the error costs twice the steps. Higher-order methods do better per NFE.

**DPM-Solver** (Lu et al., 2022). The diffusion ODE is *semi-linear*: a linear term in $x$ plus a nonlinear term through $\epsilon_\theta$. Changing variables to $\lambda_t = \log(\alpha_t/\sigma_t)$ (half the log signal-to-noise ratio; in DPM-Solver's notation $\alpha_t = \sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}$ and $\sigma_t = \sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}$) gives the exact solution
$$
x_t = \frac{\alpha_t}{\alpha_s}x_s - \alpha_t\int_{\lambda_s}^{\lambda_t} e^{-\lambda}\,\hat\epsilon_\theta(\hat x_\lambda, \lambda)\,d\lambda,
$$
so only the "exponentially weighted integral" of the network has to be approximated. The first-order version, DPM-Solver-1, is **exactly the DDIM update** (the paper's Section 4.1); second- and third-order versions evaluate the network at intermediate points. Reported: CIFAR-10 FID 4.70 with 10 NFE and 2.87 with 20 NFE, a 4–16× speed-up over earlier training-free samplers.

**EDM** (Karras et al., 2022) rewrites the ODE as $\frac{dx}{d\sigma} = \frac{x - D_\theta(x;\sigma)}{\sigma}$ with a denoiser $D_\theta$ (so $\frac{x - D_\theta}{\sigma}$ plays the role of $\epsilon_\theta$) and integrates it with **Heun's method**: an Euler step, a second evaluation at the end point, and the average of the two slopes — one extra NFE per step, local error $O(h^3)$ instead of $O(h^2)$. The last step to $\sigma = 0$ is a plain Euler step, so $N$ steps cost $2N - 1$ NFE. Step sizes follow $\sigma_i = \bigl(\sigma_{\max}^{1/\rho} + \tfrac{i}{N-1}(\sigma_{\min}^{1/\rho} - \sigma_{\max}^{1/\rho})\bigr)^\rho$ with $\rho = 7$, $\sigma_{\min} = 0.002$, $\sigma_{\max} = 80$, which concentrates steps at low noise. With $N = 18$ (35 NFE) and their improved training, EDM reached CIFAR-10 FID 1.79 class-conditional and 1.97 unconditional; applied to a previously trained ImageNet-64 network (no retraining), their sampling changes alone improved its FID from 2.07 to 1.55, and retraining with their recipe reached 1.36.

```python
# EDM Algorithm 1, deterministic Heun
for i in range(N):                            # sigmas[N] = 0
    d = (x - D(x, sigmas[i])) / sigmas[i]     # dx/dsigma at sigma_i
    x_next = x + (sigmas[i + 1] - sigmas[i]) * d
    if sigmas[i + 1] > 0:                     # 2nd-order correction
        d2 = (x_next - D(x_next, sigmas[i + 1])) / sigmas[i + 1]
        x_next = x + (sigmas[i + 1] - sigmas[i]) * 0.5 * (d + d2)
    x = x_next
```

## The toy, sampled fast

The samplers above, run on the 2-D toy model trained on the [[8-diffusion-models/ddpm#a-tiny-ddpm-you-can-run-numpy|DDPM page]] ($T = 100$; same trained weights for every sampler). Quality is the energy distance between 4,000 samples and 4,000 held-out points (unbiased estimate on 2,000-point subsamples of each); fresh real samples score $0 \pm 0.0007$, so values below about $0.001$ cannot be told apart from real data.

stochastic DDPM update (η = 1) on an evenly spaced subsequence of the 100 steps. Bottom: deterministic DDIM (η = 0) from the same starting noise. With 5–10 steps the stochastic sampler leaves visible noise around the moons; DDIM stays sharp. No retraining is involved: only the update rule and the step list change.</figcaption></figure>

stroke: var(--accent); stroke-width: 1.5; stroke-linecap: square"/></g><g><text style="font-size: 9px; font-family: 'Source Sans 3', 'Source Sans Pro', 'DejaVu Sans'; text-anchor: start; fill: currentColor" x="274.39" y="40.81" transform="rotate(-0 274.39 40.81)">η = 0 (DDIM)</text></g><g><path d="M 249.19 51.16  L 258.19 51.16  L 267.19 51.16  " style="fill: none; stroke: var(--accent2); stroke-width: 1.5; stroke-linecap: square"/></g><g><text style="font-size: 9px; font-family: 'Source Sans 3', 'Source Sans Pro', 'DejaVu Sans'; text-anchor: start; fill: currentColor" x="274.39" y="54.31" transform="rotate(-0 274.39 54.31)">Heun (2nd order)</text></g></g></g></g><defs><clipPath id="f9a9c05"><rect x="55.05" y="12.37" width="323.64" height="177.41"/></clipPath></defs></svg> <figcaption><b>Sample quality against cost</b> for the toy model (energy distance to held-out data, lower is better; hollow markers sit in the grey band where the metric cannot tell samples from real data). At 10–20 evaluations DDIM is about twice as close to the data as the stochastic update. Heun's method costs two evaluations per step: with very few steps (5–11 evaluations) its large steps go badly wrong, but at about 20 evaluations it is level with or slightly ahead of DDIM.</figcaption></figure>

| NFE | $\eta = 1$ (DDPM update) | $\eta = 0$ (DDIM) | Heun (NFE) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 5 | 0.135 | 0.090 | 59.2 (5) |
| 10 | 0.027 | 0.012 | 0.053 (11) |
| 20 | 0.0038 | 0.0019 | 0.0011 (21) |
| 50 | ≈ 0 | ≈ 0 | ≈ 0 (51) |
| 100 | ≈ 0 | ≈ 0 | ≈ 0 (101) |

For reference, the full DDPM sampler (Algorithm 2 with $\sigma_t^2 = \beta_t$, 100 NFE) scores 0.0005. Three things carry over from the papers to the toy: at 10–20 NFE DDIM is about twice as close to the data as the stochastic update; by about 50 steps every sampler is as good as the full chain; and a second-order method only pays off once its steps are small enough — with 3–6 Heun steps from $\sigma \approx 220$ down to 0 the correction extrapolates wildly. (EDM's $\rho = 7$ step spacing did not help in this toy, whose model only knows 100 discrete noise levels; uniform spacing is used throughout.)

width="167.4" height="167.4"/></clipPath></defs></svg> <figcaption><b>Starting points x<sub>T</sub> followed through 100 reverse steps.</b> Left (8 of them): the stochastic update adds fresh noise at every step, which makes the paths jagged random walks, and the same x<sub>T</sub> would land elsewhere on a rerun. Right (40): DDIM follows smooth deterministic curves (an ODE); open circles show where the same x<sub>T</sub> lands with only 10 DDIM steps, on average 0.19 away — the latent fixes the sample.</figcaption></figure>

## Training a faster sampler

Training-free solvers struggle below about 10 NFE: the ODE trajectories are curved, and a few long steps cannot follow them. The remaining approaches train a new network to take the big steps directly.

**Progressive distillation** (Salimans & Ho, 2022). Take a trained deterministic sampler with $N$ steps as the *teacher*. Initialize a *student* as a copy of the teacher and train it so that **one** of its steps lands where **two** DDIM steps of the teacher land. Then the student becomes the teacher and the process repeats with $N/2$ steps. Starting from samplers with as many as 8192 steps, they distil down to 4 steps "without losing much perceptual quality" (CIFAR-10 FID 3.0 at 4 steps), and the whole procedure costs no more than training the original model. Few-step students need care in how the network output is parameterized: predicting $\epsilon$ is unstable when $\bar\alpha_t \to 0$ (the implied $\hat x = (x_t - \sigma_t\hat\epsilon)/\alpha_t$ divides by $\alpha_t \to 0$), so the paper introduces alternatives: predicting $x$ directly, or the "velocity" $v \equiv \alpha_t\epsilon - \sigma_t x$, from which $\hat x = \alpha_t x_t - \sigma_t\hat v$ (here $\alpha_t = \sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}$, $\sigma_t = \sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}$; its App. D derives $v$ as $\mathrm dx_\phi/\mathrm d\phi$ for $x_\phi = \cos\phi\,x + \sin\phi\,\epsilon$).

**Consistency models** (Song et al., 2023). Every probability-flow-ODE trajectory ends at one data point. A consistency model $f_\theta(x_t, t)$ learns to map **any** point of a trajectory straight to that end point: $f_\theta(x_t, t) = f_\theta(x_{t'}, t')$ for points on the same trajectory (self-consistency), with the boundary condition $f_\theta(x_\epsilon, \epsilon) = x_\epsilon$ built in through $f_\theta(x, t) = c_{\text{skip}}(t)\,x + c_{\text{out}}(t)\,F_\theta(x, t)$, $c_{\text{skip}}(\epsilon) = 1$, $c_{\text{out}}(\epsilon) = 0$. Training either distils a pre-trained diffusion model (consistency distillation: make $f_\theta$ agree on two adjacent trajectory points, one obtained by a single ODE-solver step of the teacher) or needs no teacher at all (consistency training). Sampling is **one** evaluation, $\hat x = f_\theta(x_T, T)$; quality can be improved by alternating denoising with re-noising. Reported one-step FID: 3.55 on CIFAR-10 and 6.20 on ImageNet $64\times64$.

**Latent consistency models** (Luo et al., 2023) apply consistency distillation to a [[8-diffusion-models/guidance-and-latent-diffusion|latent diffusion model]] such as Stable Diffusion, including classifier-free guidance, giving $768\times768$ images in 2–4 steps after about 32 A100 GPU-hours of distillation.

## Summary

| Method | Typical NFE | Deterministic? | Retraining? |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| DDPM ancestral sampling (Ho et al. 2020) | $T$ = 1000 | no | — |
| Learned variances + fewer steps (Nichol & Dhariwal 2021) | about 50–100 | no | yes (hybrid loss) |
| DDIM, $\eta = 0$ (Song, Meng & Ermon 2020) | 20–100 | yes | **no** |
| DPM-Solver-2/3 (Lu et al. 2022) | 10–20 | yes | **no** |
| EDM Heun sampler (Karras et al. 2022) | 35 on CIFAR-10 | yes (a stochastic variant exists) | no; their training changes help further |
| Progressive distillation (Salimans & Ho 2022) | 4–8 | yes | yes, teacher → student, repeated |
| Consistency models (Song et al. 2023) | 1–2 | yes (1 step) | yes (distillation or standalone) |
| Latent consistency models (Luo et al. 2023) | 2–4 | yes | yes (distilled from a latent diffusion model) |

> [!trap] Common mistakes
> - **Notation clash.** The DDIM paper writes $\alpha_t$ for DDPM's $\bar\alpha_t$ (its $q(x_t\mid x_0) = \mathcal N(\sqrt{\alpha_t}x_0, (1-\alpha_t)I)$); DPM-Solver and progressive distillation write $\alpha_t$ for $\sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}$ and $\sigma_t$ for $\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}$. Translate before mixing formulas.
> - **Counting steps instead of NFE.** A Heun step costs two evaluations; compare samplers at equal NFE.
> - **Thinking few-step stochastic sampling needs a model trained for $S$ steps.** It does not: the DDIM paper notes that $\eta = 1$ on a subsequence of length $S$ can be seen as simulating a DDPM trained with $S$ steps, using the same network. That sampler is simply poor with large jumps (FID 41 at 10 steps above), which is the point of DDIM.
> - **Assuming deterministic means less diverse.** The randomness is all in $x_T$; different $x_T$ give different samples. What is lost is only the extra noise added *along* the path.
> - **Using a high-order solver with huge steps.** A second-order correction extrapolates from slopes that are only accurate for small steps; in the toy, Heun with 5 NFE is far worse than DDIM with 5.
> - **Forgetting $\bar\alpha_0 = 1$.** The last DDIM step goes to $t = 0$, where the "predicted $x_0$" is returned with no noise.

> [!question]- Check yourself
> 1. Why can a model trained with the DDPM loss be sampled with DDIM without retraining?
> 2. Show that for $\eta = 0$ the DDIM step is an Euler step in $(\bar x, \sigma)$ with $\bar x = x/\sqrt{\bar\alpha}$ and $\sigma = \sqrt{(1-\bar\alpha)/\bar\alpha}$.
> 3. EDM samples CIFAR-10 with 18 Heun steps. Why is that 35 NFE and not 36?
> 4. A consistency model and a 1-step DDIM sampler both use one network evaluation. Why does the consistency model produce sharp samples while 1-step DDIM does not?
>
> > [!solution]- Answers
> > 1. The loss only depends on the marginals $q(x_t\mid x_0)$; DDIM's non-Markovian processes have the same marginals and lead to the same objective (Theorem 1), so the trained $\epsilon_\theta$ fits them too.
> > 2. With $\sigma_t = 0$: $x_{t-1}/\sqrt{\bar\alpha_{t-1}} = (x_t - \sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}\epsilon_\theta)/\sqrt{\bar\alpha_t} + \sqrt{(1-\bar\alpha_{t-1})/\bar\alpha_{t-1}}\,\epsilon_\theta$, i.e. $\bar x_{t-1} = \bar x_t - \sigma_t\epsilon_\theta + \sigma_{t-1}\epsilon_\theta = \bar x_t + (\sigma_{t-1}-\sigma_t)\epsilon_\theta$.
> > 3. Each of the first 17 steps uses two evaluations; the last step goes to $\sigma = 0$, where the correction is skipped, so $2\cdot18 - 1 = 35$.
> > 4. One DDIM step returns the predicted $\hat x_0$ from pure noise, which is the *average* of all images consistent with $x_T$ — a blur. The consistency model is trained to output the end point of one specific ODE trajectory, a single sharp image.

## PyTorch docs

- [`torch.no_grad`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.no_grad.html) — wrap every sampler.
- [`torch.randn_like`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.randn_like.html) — the fresh noise $\sigma_t\epsilon_t$ (only when $\eta > 0$).
- [`torch.linspace`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.linspace.html) and [`torch.round`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.round.html) — building the step subsequence $\tau$.
- [`torch.Tensor.clamp`](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/stable/generated/torch.Tensor.clamp.html) — the `max(..., 0)` inside the square root of the direction term.
